The completion of the 2022-2031 Comprehensive Land Use Plan would not have been possible without the invaluable support, assistance, and contributions of various 
government and non-government agencies. The Municipal Government of Bamban extends its deepest gratitude to the following:
The Municipal Government of Bamban also extends its heartfelt appreciation to the President of the Bamban People’s Council, Mr. Arnaiz S. Salas and his team of Civil Society 
Organizations and to non-governmental organizations whose invaluable input and contributions during consultations greatly aided the crafting of the CLUP document. Equal 
gratitude is given to the various divisions and ofﬁces within the city government for generously sharing their time, data, and expertise.

1.​
Basic Profile 
1.1.​
Profile 
Table 1.1. Municipal Profile of Bamban, Tarlac 
Region 
III 
Province 
Tarlac 
Income Class 
Second Class 
Boundaries 
North: Municipality of Capas, Province of Tarlac 
East: Municipality of Concepcion, Province of Tarlac 
South: City of Mabalacat, Province of Pampanga 
West: Municipality of Botolan, Province of Zambales 
South West: Municipality of Porac, Province of Pampanga 
No. of Barangays 
15 
 
The municipality of Bamban is a second class municipality in the Province of Tarlac. It is located in Central Luzon, bounded on the north by the municipality of 
Capas, Tarlac, on the east by the municipality of Concepcion (Tarlac) , on the south by the municipality of Mabalacat (Pampanga), on the west by the  
municipality Botolan (Zambales), and on the southwest by the municipality of Porac (Pampanga).  
 
The municipality is politically subdivided intro 15 barangays, namely: Anupul, Banaba, Bangcu, Culubasa, Dela Cruz, La Paz, Lourdes, Malonzo, San Nicolas, 
San Pedro, San Rafael, San Roque, San Vicente, Santo Niño, Lumang Kalsada, and Virgen de los Remedios​
​
​
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies

1.2.​
History 
The history of the municipality of Bamban from its institution as a municipality during the pre-Hispanic Times to the impact of the historical eruption of Mt. 
Pinatubo in 1991  is  summarized on the table below.  
Table 1.2. List of Historical Events 
Year 
Event 
Pre-Hispanic Times 
Institution of Bamban  
1700 
Spanish Colonization Period and the spread of Roman Catholic Faith 
1876 
Establishment of Mission de Pueblos de Bamban 
Don Martin Sibal was appointed head and commissioned with the rank of Captain 
1894 
The integration of Bamban into Tarlac from Pampanga 
1896 
Revolution from Spanish conquerors 
1899 
Filipino-American revolution and proclamation of Bamban as the country’s revolutionary capital by General Emilio Aguinaldo. In recognition of 
this historical event, the Sangguniang Bayan of Bamban passed a special ordinance in 1999- the centennial anniversary of the 
proclamation-declaring June 6 as Bamban Day. 
1942 
Japanese soldiers took over the sugar central and converted it as their barracks or detention camp. 
Establishment of (KALIBAPI) Kapisanan ng Panglilingkod sa Bagong Pilipinas to gain the cooperation and good will of civilians. 
Bambanenses joined guerilla movements such as the United States Armed Forces in the Far East (USAFFE) or the Hukbong Bayan Laban sa 
Hapon (HUKBALAHAP) 
Apung Nasiong Gamboa (also known as Commander Luna) lead the #45 of HUKBALAHAP 
1944 
Liberation and Post-Liberation Years 
The entire poblacion was devastated when the American fighter planes bombed houses, schools buildings, and the public market.  
The aftermath of the war lingered for an extended period, casting a shadow over the town. This prolonged period of uncertainty and instability 
prompted influential families to make a significant decision: relocating to the city in order to rebuild their lives and businesses. The allure of the 
city's resources, opportunities, and relative safety likely played a pivotal role in their decision-making process. This migration of prominent 
families further altered the social and economic landscape of both the town and the city, marking a significant transition in the wake of the war's 
devastation. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
18 


===== PAGE 26 =====
​

​
​
​
​
 
 
Year 
Event 
1949 
 Atty. Igmedio Bolus established the first secondary school in Bamban, “Bamban Institute” 
1957 
The Holy Infant Jesus Academy was founded by Mr. Gaviola, however the management was then transferred to the Dominican Sisters and 
renamed the school into Sto. Niño Academy. 
1966 
Bamban’s First Public High School was established “The San Roque Experimental High School” now currently known as San Roque High 
School 
1972 
The detention of the Incumbent Mayor and subsequent replacement by the Vice Mayor caused significant disruption in the municipality's 
leadership. This abrupt change led to instability, uncertainty, and a loss of confidence among residents. The Vice Mayor faced challenges 
transitioning into the role of Mayor and struggled to fill the void left by their predecessor. This period of adjustment affected decision-making, 
policy implementation, and public trust. The absence of the Incumbent Mayor, who likely had strong community ties, resulted in a lack of 
leadership experience and political influence. Overall, the fallout highlighted the importance of stable leadership in local government for 
maintaining governance and stability. 
1991 
Mt. Pinatubo Eruption and Expiration of RP-US Bases Treaty 
 
the eruption of Mt. Pinatubo in 1991 devastated Bamban, affecting its economic, social, cultural, and political life. Lahar caused widespread 
destruction, submerging entire barangays and displacing residents. Two resettlement areas, Dap Dap and Mainang, were established to 
accommodate those affected. Schools were built in these areas to cater to the relocated children. 
 
The closure of the Clark Air Base due to the expiration of the RP-US Military Bases Treaty in the same year added to the economic woes, leaving 
many unemployed. However, the National Government later transformed the former military base into a special economic zone under the Clark 
Development Corporation. This led to the establishment of manufacturing plants, duty-free shops, and other businesses, creating employment 
opportunities for Bamban residents. Additionally, the Clark International Airport was developed into a major international gateway, further 
bolstering the local economy.

2.​ Demography 
2.1.​
Population Size and Growth Rate 
The population of Bamban has experienced notable fluctuations in growth over the past century. In 2022, the population declined to 74,292 with a -2.54% 
Annual Population Growth Rate (APGR), likely due to migration during the COVID-19 pandemic. Before this, in 2020, the population reached 78,260 with a 
2.41% growth rate. The highest growth occurred between 2000 and 2007, with APGRs of 4.55% and 4.15%, driven by urbanization. Growth slowed between 
1995 to 2000 and 2010 to 2015, averaging 0.81% to 2.16%. The mid-20th century also saw rapid expansion, particularly from 1960 to 1975, peaking at 4.23%.

2.3.​
Population Density by Barangay 
Based on the latest 2020 PSA Census, the  population density for the Municipality of Bamban is 311 persons per square kilometer or 3.11 persons per hectare. 
The population density was calculated using the total population and the land area as of December 2013 which was based on LMB and DENR and as certified by 
DBM. Compared to the figures in 2020, there was a 25.4% increase  from population density of 2010 and 12.7% increase from 2015 figures.  The population 
density of Bamban is generally lower than the average population density of the province of Tarlac.

2.5.​
Population Per Age Group 
Based on the 2020 Census, the productive population of the municipality of Bamban was recorded at 63.59 percent, while the 2022 CBMS recorded only 33.66 
percent of the total population as part of the productive group. The total dependency ratio was 36.41 percent in the 2020 Census and 33.26 percent in the 2022 
CBMS. 
 
This dependency ratio was a 32.03 percent child-dependency ratio and a 4.39 percent old-age dependency ratio in the 2020 Census. In comparison, the 2022 
CBMS recorded a 28.12 percent child-dependency ratio and a 5.14 percent old-age dependency ratio.

2.7.​
Total Population by Sex, Employment and Unemployment Status 
The 2022 labor force data for Bamban reveals significant gender disparity, with males comprising 67.5% of the workforce and females 32.5%. While employment 
is high at 90.2%, key barangays like Anupul and San Nicolas act as employment hubs. However, 2,518 people are unemployed, with females facing higher rates, 
especially in Bangcu. Additionally, 959 individuals are underemployed, mainly in San Nicolas, Anupul, and Lourdes.

2.9.​
Employed Persons 15 Yrs. Old and Over by Occupation Group 
2.9.1.​
Industry Group 
The 2022 labor force data for Bamban shows distinct gender differences across industries. Males dominate Agriculture, Forestry, and Fishing and Construction, 
while Manufacturing sees notable female participation at 37.9%. Wholesale and Retail Trade has a balanced gender representation. In contrast, women are the 
majority in Education and Human Health and Social Work, playing a prominent role in these sectors.

2.15.​
Population by Marital Status 
Based on the 2022 CBMS, 37.34% of the household population are married, while 42.43% are single.  Among the age groups, the 40-44 and the 30-34 age groups  
have the most number of married individuals or about 66% of the 40-44 age group population, and 49.5% of the 30-34 population.

2.16.​
Total Population by Mother Tongue/Ethnicity 
The 2022 population data for Bamban shows a total of 74,292 individuals from diverse ethnic groups. The largest group is Kapampangan, with 58,588 members, 
followed by Tagalog (8,259), Aeta (1,483), Bisaya/Binisaya (1,414), and Ilocano (1,006). Smaller communities include Aeta/Ayta-Mang-Ansti (959) and Ilonggo 
(222). Additionally, various foreign ethnicities, such as American, Chinese, and Japanese, are present, albeit in smaller numbers, reflecting the rich ethnic diversity 
of Bamban.

2.17.​
Total Population by Religious Affiliation 
The 2022 religious affiliation data for Bamban highlights its diverse population of 74,292 residents. The predominant group is Roman Catholics, numbering 
62,415, including both charismatic and non-charismatic members. Significant other affiliations include Iglesia Ni Cristo (3,848), Filipino Assemblies of the First 
Born (1,195), and various Evangelical churches, such as the Alliance of Bible Christian Communities (1,652). The Muslim community comprises 170 
individuals, and 680 residents report no religious affiliation. This data reflects the community's rich cultural and spiritual diversity.

2.18.​
Disabled persons by Barangay, Sex 
The data reveals that in 2022, 385 individuals with disabilities were distributed across Bamban's barangays, with Anupul (99) and San Nicolas (96) having the 
highest counts. In contrast, barangays like Bangcu (1) and Culubasa (2) reported very few cases. San Roque (54) and Lourdes (42) also had notable numbers, 
while other areas recorded minimal cases.

2.20.​
No. of Households Projection 
The household projection was derived using the average household size of 3.90 persons per household, based on the 2022 CBMS data. By 2031, the number of 
urban households in Bamban is projected to reach 20,977, while rural households are expected to number 6,018. 
 
By the end of the planning period in 2031, the number of households in Bamban is projected to be at 26,995.

2.22.​
Population Projection Per Group 
By 2032, the projected labor force is expected to reach 61,679, while the dependent population is estimated at 25,250, consisting of 18,716 young dependents and 
6,533 elderly dependents. As a result, the dependency ratio at the end of the planning period is anticipated to be approximately 29%.

2.23.​
Fertility Rate 
The Total Fertility Rate (TFR) experienced a notable decline from 2020 to 2022. In 2020, the TFR was 69.23%, with 1,082 live births, but it decreased to 58.19% 
in 2021 with 931 births, and further dropped to 42.91% in 2022 with 638 births. This downward trend reflects a change in reproductive behavior, likely 
influenced by successful family planning programs and greater awareness of reproductive health. The falling fertility rate points toward population stabilization, 
easing pressure on social services and promoting sustainable development. 
Table 2.23 Fertility Rate

2.24.​
Crude Birth Rate 
The table shows a significant decline in the Crude Birth Rate (CBR) from 2020 to 2022, with live births dropping from 1,082 in 2020 (CBR: 13.83%) to 638 in 
2022 (CBR: 8.58%). This decrease reflects a shift in fertility patterns, largely attributed to successful family planning initiatives like the mother’s class and family 
counseling, which have raised awareness about controlling family size and birth spacing. As a result, families are more proactive in managing household planning.

2.25.​
Crude Death Rate 
The table illustrates a steady decline in the Death Rate from 2020 to 2022. In 2020, there were 567 recorded deaths, resulting in a death rate of 7.25%. This was 
followed by a decrease in 2021, with 445 deaths and a death rate of 5.56%. By 2022, the number of deaths further dropped to 336, bringing the death rate down 
to 4.52%. This consistent decline in the death rate over the three-year period indicates an improvement in overall health conditions and enhanced healthcare 
services in the municipality and successful public health initiatives.

2.26.​
Labor Force Participation by Sex 
The 2022 labor force participation statistics for Bamban indicate a total population of 53,386, with 25,601 individuals engaged in the labor force. Out of these, 
17,274 are male and 8,327 are female. In contrast, 27,780 individuals are not participating in the labor force, including 9,673 males and 18,107 females. There are 
also 5 individuals whose participation status is unreported.

2.27.​
Unemployment Rate, Total and Sex 
Based on 2022 CBMS, Bamban recorded an overall unemployment rate of 9.84%, with 2,518 individuals unemployed out of 25,601 in the labor force. The data 
highlights significant gender disparities, as the female unemployment rate (16.07%) was notably higher than the male unemployment rate (6.83%), despite fewer 
women participating in the labor force (8,327 females vs. 17,274 males). Barangays Bangcu (128.39%) and Malonzo (121.43%) showed extreme unemployment 
rates due to their small labor force and high number of unemployed individuals, while Culubasa (4.17%) and Virgen de los Remedios (5.24%) had the lowest 
rates.

2.28.​
Average Family Income 
The 2022 CBMS data shows that most households in Bamban, Tarlac have low to middle incomes, with the majority (8,723 households) earning between 11,000 
and 21,999 pesos monthly. Lower income brackets also account for significant portions, while only 129 households earn 76,000 pesos or more, indicating few 
higher-income earners. This reflects a predominantly low to middle-income community.

2.29.​
Annual Population Growth Rate 
The population data for Bamban indicates fluctuations from 2020 to 2026. In 2020, the population was 78,260, showing moderate growth to 80,051 in 2021 
(2.29%). However, a significant decline occurred in 2022, dropping to 74,330 (-7.15%), attributed to inconsistencies in the 2022 CBMS release, likely due to the 
pandemic. From 2023 onwards, the population is projected to grow steadily, reaching 89,639 by 2026, with a consistent growth rate of 2.29%.

3.1.5.​
Probable Source of Pollution  
A.​
Agricultural Activities: 
●​ Chemical Use: The use of fertilizers and pesticides in farming can lead to soil and water pollution, affecting both local ecosystems and human 
health. Proper management and regulation of these chemicals are essential to mitigate their impact. 
●​ Livestock Waste: Improper disposal of manure from livestock can contaminate water sources and soil, leading to environmental 
degradation and health risks. 
B.​
 Industrial Activities: 
●​ Small-Scale Manufacturing: Local industries may generate waste and emissions that contribute to air and water pollution. Implementing 
environmental regulations and waste management practices can help reduce these impacts. 
●​ Construction Activities: Dust and noise pollution from construction sites can affect air quality and community health. 
C.​
Transportation: 
●​ Vehicle Emissions: National and Provincial Highway traffic, as well from nearby highway such as the SCTEX, contributes to air pollution. 
Encouraging cleaner transportation options and improving traffic management can help mitigate this issue. 
●​ Infrastructure Development: Noise and dust from ongoing infrastructure projects can impact local air quality and community well-being. 
D.​
Domestic and Municipal Waste: 
●​ Untreated Sewage: Inadequate sewage systems can lead to water pollution, posing health risks to residents. Improving waste treatment facilities is 
crucial. 
●​ Improper Waste Disposal: Open dumping of household waste contributes to soil and air pollution, necessitating effective waste management 
strategies. 
E.​
Natural Sources: 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
106 


===== PAGE 114 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
●​ Flooding: Natural flooding can carry pollutants into water sources, affecting water quality. Implementing flood control measures and maintaining 
natural buffers can help mitigate this risk. 
●​ Geologic Risks: Bamban's geology may pose risks such as landslides or soil instability, which can lead to environmental degradation if not 
properly managed. 
F.​
Infrastructure and Development Projects: 
●​ Construction Noise: Noise pollution from ongoing infrastructure projects can affect community health and quality of life. 
●​ Dust and Particulates: Air pollution from construction activities can exacerbate respiratory issues among residents.

4.1.3.​
Backlog 
The housing backlog in the municipality is composed entirely of doubled-up and displaced households, as there are no recorded homeless individuals or 
households. Doubled-up households, or those where multiple households share a single dwelling unit, total 2,836. Meanwhile, displaced households—those 
residing in hazard-prone areas, locations designated for priority infrastructure projects, or areas subject to court-ordered demolition—amount to 2,797. This 
distribution indicates that displaced households make up 49.65% of the backlog, while doubled-up households account for 50.35%. To fully address the housing 
backlog over the 10-year program period, a total of 5,633 new housing units will be required.

4.6.7.​
Ten Leading Causes of Morbidity for the Last Three Years 
The morbidity data for Bamban from 2020 to 2022 identifies the top 10 disease causes. Acute respiratory infections were notably prevalent, affecting 373 
individuals in 2022, a slight increase from 372 in 2021 and 367 in 2020. Post-traumatic wound infections also rose, from 237 cases in 2020 to 240 in 2022. Acute 
upper respiratory infections averaged around 140 cases in 2022, while urinary tract infections and open wounds remained stable at 65 and 64 cases, respectively. 
The data highlights ongoing concerns with respiratory and bacterial infections, along with skin abscesses, dizziness, and unspecified allergies as significant health 
issues in the community.

4.6.8.​
Ten Leading Causes of Mortality for the Last Three Years 
The Bamban mortality statistics from 2020 to 2022 shows notable variations in the death toll. From 140 in 2020 to 717 in 2021 and then to 322 in 2022, the total 
number of deaths increased. Infant mortality jumped from three in 2021 to five in 2022, while child mortality under five years old grew to four instances in 2021 
and eleven in 2022. There were six non-communicable disease-related deaths in 2022, while no maternal, neonatal, or foetal deaths were reported. Three people 
lost their lives in traffic-related events in 2021. Overall, the data indicates that mortality rates peaked in 2021 and then began to fall in 2022.

4.6.9.​
Malnourished Children for the last Three Years Malnutrition 
A comparison of Bamban's malnutrition data for 2019 and 2021 shows how many children are underweight or severely underweight. The overall number of cases 
in the 0–5 month age group dropped from 18 in 2019 to 10 in 2021. Malnutrition cases for children between the ages of 6 and 11 months increased from 7 in 
2019 to 18 in 2021, with a considerable increase in the female cohort. Malnutrition cases in the 12- to 23-month age range increased significantly, from 58 in 2019 
to 90 in 2021. The overall count of undernourished children between the ages of 0 and 59 months rose from 253 in 2019 to 318 in 2020 before falling to 163 in 
2021. This information shows a worrisome increase in child malnutrition, especially with older babies and toddlers.

4.8.​
Number of Households in Occupied  Housing Units by Type of Toilet Facilities 
According to the CBMS 2022 data for Bamban, out of the 19,047 homes examined, 14,600 of them used the flush to septic tank toilet facility, making it the most 
common. Furthermore, a piped sewer system was linked to 3,013 homes. Fewer people used open drains (60) or flush toilets that led to pit latrines (793). The 
toilet connections were unknown to only three families. There were also bucket toilets (95), composting toilets (25), and slab pit latrines (24). While 199 families 
were served by public restrooms, just one household reported utilising a hanging toilet. 234 homes, notably, were without adequate facilities and had to rely on 
the fields or the bush. 
 
 Table 4.8 Households in occupied housing units by type of toilet facilities 2022

4.9.​
Educational Facilities 
The Public Day Care for Primary level students are located at the different barangays. Each centre has one classroom to facilitate early childhood education. With 
nine centres, Anupul has the most, followed by Banaba with two. There are several centres in other barangays as well, such as San Nicolas, Lourdes (Dapdap), and 
San Roque. These facilities span between 0.005 and 0.054 hectares of land.

4.9.13.​
Student - Teacher and Student - Classroom Ratio by Level (Public) 
The statistics on classroom and student-teacher ratios show enrolment and staffing at all educational levels. The student-teacher ratio at the preparatory level is 
35:1, with 1,596 pupils enrolled. 9,706 pupils are enrolled in the elementary school, which likewise maintains a 35:1 ratio. There are 2,550 pupils in senior high 
school with the same ratio of 40:1, compared to 5,370 in junior high school. All things considered, the data point to an educational system-wide commitment to 
reasonable class sizes.

4.9.17.​
Historical Enrollment Participation Rate for the Last Five (5) Years 
Overall, there is a growing trend in student engagement, according to the Enrollment Participation Rate (EPR) for Bamban, Tarlac across various educational 
levels has shown fluctuations over the past five school years. In Preparatory, the EPR rose from 70.65% in 2017-2018 to 91.00% in 2021-2022, peaking during the 
last year. Elementary level enrollment was similarly strong, with an EPR starting at 78.54% in 2017-2018, reaching a high of 96.74% in 2020-2021, before slightly 
decreasing to 96.50% in the following year. Junior High School saw a gradual increase from 77.19% in 2017-2018 to 92.03% in 2021-2022, indicating consistent 
growth. Meanwhile, Senior High School experienced an increase from 75.94% in 2017-2018 to 96.32% in 2021-2022, showing significant improvement. Tertiary 
enrollment fluctuated, starting at 79.18% in 2017-2018, dipping to 74.61% in 2020-2021, but rising again to 78.79% in 2021-2022.

5.​ Economic Sector 
5.1.​
Profile 
5.1.1.​
Land Use 
Majority of the agricultural lands in the municipality of Bamban are considered as production agricultural lands. There are approximately 3,352.00  hectares 
dedicated for  agricultural use 1708.71 hectares fall under production areas, while 1,308.42 hectares are protected agricultural land.

5.2.8.​
Agrarian Related Concerns 
CARP‑covered lands exist across 12 barangays in Bamban under the Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Program (CARP), managed by the Department of 
Agrarian Reform (DAR). The table below details both the total area designated for CARP and the locations of Agrarian Reform Communities (ARCs). 
Notably, Barangay Sto. Niño still has 37.5 hectares pending coverage under the program. This reflects DAR’s ongoing efforts to redistribute all eligible 
agricultural land—referred to as “CARPable” land, which is certified by DAR and DENR for acquisition and distribution to farmer-beneficiaries

5.3.3.​
Local Revenue and Industrial Establishment for the last 5 years 
The data reveals significant fluctuations in local revenue and employment for industrial establishments over five years. Revenue and employment rose notably 
from 2019 to 2020, with a ₱1 million increase and 45 additional jobs, likely due to economic growth. However, 2021 saw a sharp drop, with revenue plummeting 
by ₱2.5 million and 112 jobs lost, mainly due to COVID-19 restrictions. Recovery began in 2022, with revenue and employment both rebounding significantly. 
This positive trend continued in 2023, with further gains in both areas, signaling a steady economic recovery.

5.4.2.​
Historical Data on Commercial Areas 
The data shows significant commercial expansion in most barangays from 2021 to 2022. Anupul remains the largest commercial area, while San Roque, San Nicolas, and 
Lourdes experienced the most growth. Smaller barangays like Banaba, La Paz, and San Vicente also saw modest increases. In contrast, Bangcu, Malonzo, and San Rafael showed 
no changes, indicating limited commercial development in these areas.

5.4.3.​
Number of Business Permits Issued by Type 
The number of business permits issued in Bamban declined from 963 in 2021 to 747 in 2022. The tertiary sector, which dominates the business landscape, saw a 
notable decrease in permits from 810 to 620. The secondary sector, including manufacturing and utilities, also experienced a reduction, especially in 
construction. The primary sector remained stable with minimal changes.

5.4.4.​
Area and Location of Forestlands by Sub-Category and Primary Use 
Based on the Forest Land Use Plan (FLUP) of Bamban, the municipality has identified specific sub-categories for the use and management of its forestlands. 
These areas are classified according to their primary use, which supports both environmental sustainability and economic development goals. Below is an 
expanded discussion of the areas and locations by sub-category and primary use:
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
203 


===== PAGE 211 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
5.4.4.1.​
Bamboo  (1,442.23 hectares) 
 
Primary Use: Bamboo Farming and Conservation 
●​ Area: 1,442.23 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: The bamboo areas are designated for the cultivation of bamboo, which has significant economic and environmental benefits. Bamboo is a 
versatile resource used in construction, crafts, furniture, paper, and other industries. It is also important for soil conservation, as bamboo helps in 
preventing erosion and maintaining water quality in river systems.​
 
Location: The bamboo forests in Bamban are likely situated in areas where the topography is suitable for bamboo growth, typically in river valleys or areas with 
moderate slopes. These areas are strategically located to ensure access to both water and fertile soil conditions necessary for bamboo cultivation. 
Economic Benefits: Bamboo farming can provide income opportunities for local communities, especially in terms of livelihood projects, which can support 
sustainable livelihoods for farmers in Bamban. 
 
5.4.4.2.​
Agroforestry (398.88 hectares) 
 
Primary Use: Agroforestry Systems for Integrated Farming 
●​ Area: 398.88 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: Agroforestry systems combine trees and crops in a way that benefits both the environment and the economy. In Bamban, these lands are 
designated for mixed farming, where farmers integrate tree crops such as fruit trees or timber species with food crops or other agricultural activities. This 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
204 


===== PAGE 212 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
system promotes soil fertility, reduces erosion, and enhances biodiversity while generating income.​
 
Location: Agroforestry areas are likely situated on slopes or areas prone to erosion, where trees can help stabilize the soil and prevent landslides. These areas are 
also expected to be located near agricultural zones where farmers can integrate agroforestry with traditional farming practices. 
Benefits: Agroforestry systems increase overall farm productivity and diversify sources of income for farmers. In addition, agroforestry can contribute to carbon 
sequestration, improving the municipality’s climate resilience. 
 
5.4.4.3.​
 Reforestation (1,887.81 hectares) 
 
Primary Use: Forest Restoration and Rehabilitation 
●​ Area: 1,887.81 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: Reforestation involves planting trees in areas where the natural forest cover has been depleted or degraded. This area is designated for the 
restoration of Bamban’s forests, which will help enhance the municipality's environmental sustainability, improve watershed functions, and provide 
wildlife habitat. The reforestation initiative also aims to restore biodiversity and ensure water and soil conservation.​
 
Location: Reforestation efforts are likely focused on denuded or degraded lands, particularly in areas previously subjected to logging or agricultural expansion. 
These areas are typically located in upland and forested regions of Bamban, especially those that are prone to soil erosion or flooding. 
Environmental and Ecological Benefits: The reforestation project will contribute to maintaining the ecological balance of the region, improving air quality, and 
protecting biodiversity. The newly planted forests will also help in carbon sequestration, contributing to the municipality’s efforts to combat climate change. 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
205 


===== PAGE 213 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
5.4.4.4.​
Cacao (250 hectares) 
 
Primary Use: Cacao Farming 
●​ Area: 250 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: The cacao areas are specifically designated for the cultivation of cacao trees, which are essential for the chocolate industry. Cacao farming in 
Bamban aims to support both the local economy and the national agricultural sector, as cacao is a high-demand crop. Cacao farming also fits well into 
agroforestry systems, where farmers can integrate it with other crops.​
 
Location: Cacao farms are likely to be situated in areas with rich, well-drained soils and favorable climate conditions, typically in lower-elevation areas with 
adequate rainfall. These areas may be found in the central and eastern parts of Bamban, which are more suitable for such tropical crops. 
Economic and Social Benefits: Cacao farming will provide a source of income for local farmers and can foster local agro-processing industries. Bamban could 
become a key supplier of high-quality cacao, contributing to the local and national chocolate production industries. 
5.4.4.5.​
Coffee (305 hectares) 
 
Primary Use: Coffee Farming 
●​ Area: 305 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: Coffee is another high-value crop that the municipality plans to cultivate. Coffee farming supports local farmers and contributes to the national 
coffee industry, which is a significant sector of the agricultural economy. Like cacao, coffee can be integrated into agroforestry systems, benefiting from 
shade provided by trees.​
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
206 


===== PAGE 214 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
Location: Coffee farms in Bamban will likely be located in areas with higher elevations and moderate slopes, where the cool climate is ideal for coffee cultivation. 
These areas are typically in the uplands of Bamban, where elevation provides favorable conditions for growing quality coffee beans. 
Economic Potential: The coffee sector in Bamban could attract investments in processing plants and create new market opportunities for local farmers. It would 
also be valuable to explore specialty coffee production, which has a growing demand in both local and international markets. 
5.4.4.6.​
 Fuelwood (240 hectares) 
Primary Use: Fuelwood Production 
●​ Area: 240 hectares​
 
●​ Purpose: Fuelwood is an important resource for many rural communities in the Philippines, particularly for cooking and heating purposes. The 
240-hectare area designated for fuelwood production will allow Bamban to sustainably meet the fuelwood needs of its communities while protecting 
natural forests from overharvesting.​
 
Location: Fuelwood plantations are typically located in areas where access to wood for cooking and heating is essential, often close to rural settlements or 
communities. These areas may be situated near major roads or in peripheral areas of Bamban, where fuelwood can be harvested sustainably. 
Social and Economic Benefits: Providing a sustainable source of fuelwood can reduce pressure on natural forests and promote the use of alternative energy 
sources. It also offers economic opportunities for local communities engaged in the production and sale of fuelwood. 
Bamban’s Forest Land Use Plan outlines a strategic approach to land management that balances environmental protection with economic development. The 
various sub-categories—Bamboo, Agroforestry, Reforestation, Cacao, Coffee, and Fuelwood—serve different purposes, from restoring degraded lands to creating 
opportunities for sustainable farming and resource management. The plan aims to ensure that Bamban’s natural resources are utilized efficiently while 
contributing to both local livelihoods and broader environmental goals. 
The designated areas for these uses are strategically located in regions best suited to each crop or activity, ensuring that Bamban can maximize its agricultural 
potential while protecting its forest ecosystems

5.4.7.​
Employment by Type/Classification/Type of Business and Trade 
According to Bamban's employment data, 5,974 people are employed in the primary, secondary, and tertiary sectors, bringing in Php 10.63 million. With 3,798 
workers and Php 4.32 million in income, the tertiary sector is the largest employing, driven primarily by trade, hotels, and services. With 1,253 employees, the 
primary sector produces Php 2.64 million, while the secondary sector employs 923 people and contributes Php 3.67 million.

5.5.1.​
Local Revenue and Employment by Tourism Activities for the Past Five Years 
The local tourism industry's earnings and employment levels in Bamban have varied dramatically during the last five years. Local tourism generated PHP 659,105.98 in revenue 
in 2019, supporting 159 jobs. But when the COVID-19 pandemic struck in 2020, business suffered a severe setback that resulted in revenue falling to PHP 76,104.31 and a 
workforce of just 23 people. In 2021, a gradual rebound started, resulting in a rise in sales to PHP 210,141.85 and employment to 81. With 93 jobs and a revenue of PHP 
376,524.23 by 2022, domestic tourist gains helped the industry recuperate. By 2023, it employed 138 people and generated PHP 547,942.46 in revenue, mostly from cultural 
events and eco-tourism. 
 
Table 5.5.1  Local Revenue and Employment by Tourism Activities for the Past Five Years

5.7.​
National and local policies on industrial development 
5.7.1.​
Local Incentives 
Bamban Municipal Investment Incentive Code of 2009 
●​ Purpose: This ordinance aims to promote economic development in Bamban by attracting investments through incentives.​
 
●​ Bamban Investment Incentive Board (BIIB): This board oversees the implementation of policies and incentives to encourage businesses to invest in 
the municipality.​
 
●​ Possible Incentives:​
 
○​ Tax exemptions or reductions for new investors.​
 
○​ Assistance in processing business permits.​
 
○​ Other non-fiscal incentives to facilitate ease of doing business in the municipality. 
5.7.2.​
 Municipal Ordinance No. 093-2022: Enacted in 2022, this ordinance grants tax credits or discounts to individuals and entities financing 
or contributing to priority projects within the municipality ​ 
●​ Purpose: Supports individuals and businesses that finance or contribute to priority projects within Bamban.​
 
●​ Incentives Offered:​
 
○​ Tax credits or discounts on local business taxes and fees.​
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
243 


===== PAGE 251 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
○​ Preferential treatment in municipal programs for companies engaged in high-impact development projects (such as infrastructure, health, and 
education initiatives).​
 
●​ Eligibility: Businesses or individuals must prove their contributions to designated municipal projects. 
 
5.7.3.​
National Incentives 
5.7.3.1.​
Philippine Economic Zone Authority (PEZA) 
Businesses operating within PEZA-designated zones benefit from incentives such as income tax holidays, tax and duty-free importation of capital equipment, and 
other fiscal incentives. ​ 
 
-​
Scope: PEZA provides incentives to businesses operating within PEZA-designated economic zones, which include manufacturing, IT services, 
agro-industrial, and tourism enterprises.​
 
-​
Key Incentives:​
 
●​ Income Tax Holiday (ITH): Up to 6 years of exemption from corporate income tax.​
 
●​ Duty-Free Importation: No taxes on imported capital equipment, raw materials, and production supplies.​
 
●​ 5% Gross Income Tax Option: After the ITH period, businesses may opt to pay a lower 5% tax on gross income instead of regular corporate 
taxes.​
 
●​ Simplified Import-Export Procedures: No import duties on materials used in export production. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
244 


===== PAGE 252 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
5.7.3.2.​
 Board of Investments (BOI) 
-​
Scope: The BOI, under the Department of Trade and Industry (DTI), grants incentives to businesses engaged in priority investment areas identified in 
the Strategic Investment Priorities Plan (SIPP).​
 
-​
Key Incentives: 
●​ Income Tax Holiday (ITH): 
○​ 4-6 years for new projects. 
○​ Additional years for projects in less-developed areas like certain regions of Tarlac. 
●​ Enhanced Deductions: 
○​ Additional tax deductions for labor expenses and research & development. 
●​ Import Duty Exemptions: 
○​ Tax-free importation of raw materials and capital equipment. 
5.7.3.3.​
 Regional Agri-Industrial Centers (RAICs) 
-​
Purpose: RAICs are special agriculture-focused industrial zones that promote agribusiness, food processing, and rural development.​
 
-​
Key Incentives:​
 
●​ Government-backed financial assistance for agribusiness startups.​
 
●​ Infrastructure support (roads, irrigation, post-harvest facilities) for businesses operating within RAICs.​
 
●​ Access to subsidized credit programs from government financing institutions.​
 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies 
245 


===== PAGE 253 =====
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
​
 
 
5.7.4.​
Special Economic Zone in Bamban 
Proclamation No. 1035 (Clark Special Economic Zone Extension) 
●​ Scope: This proclamation designates portions of Bamban, Tarlac (along with Capas, Angeles, Mabalacat, and Porac) as part of the Clark Special 
Economic Zone (CSEZ).​
 
●​ Key Incentives: 
○​ Tax Holidays: Income tax exemption for businesses within the zone. 
○​ Customs and Tax-Free Importation: No duties on imported goods for manufacturing, processing, and exports. 
○​ Infrastructure and Utility Incentives: Reduced costs for electricity, water, and telecommunications.​
 
●​ Eligible Businesses: 
○​ Manufacturing and assembly plants. 
○​ IT and business process outsourcing (BPO) firms. 
○​ Logistics and warehousing companies. 
○​ Tourism and leisure facilities. 
The Municipality of Bamban benefits from a combination of local and national investment incentives aimed at attracting businesses in industrial, agricultural, 
and special economic zones. Entrepreneurs and investors can take advantage of tax exemptions, duty-free importation, financial assistance, and infrastructure 
support to establish and grow their businesses in the area. 
If you need assistance in applying for these incentives, it's best to coordinate with the Bamban Investment Incentive Board (BIIB), PEZA, or BOI to ensure 
eligibility and compliance with requirements. 
 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies

6.2.2.​
Road Inventory Summary 
The road inventory summary indicates a total of 233.696 kilometers of roads, with urban barangay roads representing 60.19% (141.405 km) and rural barangay 
roads at 23.89% (56.076 km). National roads make up 2.54% (5.970 km), provincial roads account for 8.08% (18.965 km), and private roads represent 4.80% 
(11.280 km).

6.2.6.​
 Transport Related Projects 
The Municipality of Bamban envisions the development of a strategic Intermodal Transport Hub, capitalizing on its central location between two of the 
Philippines' major economic zones. This hub aims to streamline transportation in the area by connecting various modes of transport, reducing traffic congestion, 
and improving overall mobility. The facility will seamlessly integrate the North-South Commuter Railway, a Public Bus Station, a Jeepney Terminal, a 
Tricycle Terminal, and a Pay Parking Facility, creating an efficient, multi-modal transport network that ensures smoother transitions for passengers. This 
initiative is crucial in addressing current transport challenges while supporting Bamban's growth and economic development. 
 
Bamban Comprehensive Land Use Plan (2022-2031) Volume 3: Sectoral Studies

7.1.2.​
Inventory of Government Land 
The absence of an official Inventory of Government Land in Bamban can be elaborated upon by considering the following detailed factors shaping the land 
tenure situation in the municipality: 
 
1. Predominance of Private Land Ownership: 
 
A significant portion of Bamban's land is privately owned, which is rooted in the municipality's historical land distribution. Over time, land has primarily been 
divided and transferred within families or sold to private individuals rather than being acquired by the government. This pattern of private ownership has shaped 
the development trajectory, focusing more on private investments rather than public land acquisitions. As a result, there are limited public landholdings, and the 
local government has not prioritized the establishment of a government land inventory. With most land under private ownership, efforts have been geared toward 
regulating private land use rather than consolidating public land for government projects. 
 
2. Significant Coverage of Ancestral Domain (CADT): 
 
About 50.6% of Bamban's total land area, approximately 10,684 hectares out of 21,120 hectares, falls within the boundaries of a Certificate of Ancestral Domain 
Title (CADT) awarded to the indigenous Aeta community. This land is managed by the indigenous peoples in accordance with their customs and traditional 
practices, and it is legally protected under the Indigenous Peoples' Rights Act (IPRA). The recognition of the CADT restricts the local government's authority 
over these areas, as any use or development within the CADT lands must align with the community’s consent and cultural practices. This substantial coverage 
further limits the availability of land for government use, making it challenging to establish a comprehensive inventory of public land for municipal projects. 
 
Source: Assessor’s office​
